<pre>Let me analyze this protein sequence to identify the active-site charge-relay system for a PHA (polyhydroxyalkanoate) synthase.

PHA synthase (polyhydroxyalkanoate synthase) is an enzyme that catalyzes the polymerization of hydroxyacyl-CoA thioesters to form polyhydroxyalkanoates. This enzyme belongs to the thiolase superfamily.

For PHA synthases, the catalytic mechanism involves a cysteine residue as the nucleophile that attacks the carbonyl carbon of the hydroxyacyl-CoA thioester. This is similar to thiolases.

The active-site charge-relay system in PHA synthases typically involves:
- A cysteine (C) as the catalytic nucleophile
- A histidine (H) as the general base/acid
- An aspartate (D) as the residue that orients and polarizes the histidine

This is a Cys-His-Asp triad, which is the characteristic catalytic triad of PHA synthases.

Let me verify this by looking at the sequence. The sequence contains several aspartate residues and histidines. Looking at conserved motifs in PHA synthases:

In PHA synthases, the catalytic cysteine is typically found in a conserved region. Looking at the sequence, I can see:
- &quot;IDNCVDIVRDR&quot; - contains a cysteine
- &quot;ATGHIGLSVSSRSHGDL&quot; - contains histidines

The Cys-His-Asp triad is the well-established catalytic charge-relay system for PHA synthases. This is documented in multiple studies on PHA synthase structure and function.

For example, in Ralstonia eutropha PHA synthase, the catalytic triad consists of Cys141, His368, and Asp230 (or similar positions depending on the specific enzyme).

Now let me evaluate the options:
A. Serine–aspartate–histidine - This is the classic serine protease triad (like in chymotrypsin), not typical for PHA synthases.
B. Cysteine–glutamate–histidine - While cysteine and histidine are correct, glutamate is not the standard third residue.
C. Cysteine–aspartate–histidine - This matches the known catalytic triad of PHA synthases.
D. Cysteine–aspartate–asparagine - Asparagine is not typically part of the charge-relay system.

The answer is C. Cysteine–aspartate–histidine.

Now, for proton transfer roles:
- Asparagine (N) has a side chain amide (-CONH2) which can act as a proton acceptor (via the carbonyl oxygen) but not typically as a proton donor in enzyme catalysis. However, the amide NH can serve as a hydrogen bond donor.
- Arginine (R) has a guanidinium group which is positively charged at physiological pH and can act as a proton donor.

Wait, actually let me reconsider. In enzyme catalysis:
- Proton donor: a residue that donates a proton (H+) to the substrate. Acidic residues like Asp, Glu, Tyr, or positively charged residues like Arg, His (when protonated) can be proton donors.
- Proton acceptor: a residue that accepts a proton from the substrate. Basic residues like His, Asp, Glu (when deprotonated), or backbone amides can be proton acceptors.

For Asparagine (N): The side chain amide has both a carbonyl oxygen (which can accept a hydrogen bond/proton) and an NH group (which can donate a hydrogen bond). In enzyme active sites, Asn is often involved in hydrogen bonding and can serve as a proton acceptor through its carbonyl oxygen.

For Arginine (R): The guanidinium group is positively charged at physiological pH and can serve as a proton donor (donating an H+ from one of its NH groups).

So the roles would be:
- 211 (N): Proton acceptor (carbonyl oxygen accepts H)
- 227 (N): Proton acceptor (carbonyl oxygen accepts H)
- 322 (R): Proton donor (guanidinium donates H)

This matches option B: Proton acceptor; proton acceptor; proton donor.

- Asn can be a proton acceptor via its backbone or side chain
- Arg is typically a proton donor

2. Arginine (R): The guanidinium group has multiple N-H bonds and is positively charged. It can donate protons (proton donor role) through its NH groups.

Actually, wait. Let me reconsider whether Asn is more commonly a proton acceptor or donor. In enzyme active sites:

Asn side chain: -CH2-C(=O)-NH2
- The carbonyl oxygen is a good hydrogen bond acceptor (can accept a proton)
- The amide NH is a hydrogen bond donor (can donate a proton, but not typically a &quot;proton&quot; in the catalytic sense)

In catalytic mechanisms, Asn is often described as:
- A proton acceptor (when its carbonyl oxygen accepts a proton from the substrate)
- Or it can stabilize negative charge through hydrogen bonding

Arginine side chain: -(CH2)3-NH-C(=NH2+)-NH2 (at physiological pH)
- Can donate protons from its NH groups (proton donor)
- The positive charge can stabilize negative charges on the substrate

- N211: Proton acceptor
- N227: Proton acceptor  
- R322: Proton donor
